RATING CHANGE, logged per the thesis-drift rule: Rothschild & Co / Redburn DOWNGRADES SHOP TO NEUTRAL (from Buy).** **What makes this worth more than a rating line is that the downgrade is entirely derivative of a [[META]] thesis — Redburn's new Internet analyst (Dominic Ball) launched 100+ pages arguing Meta's end-to-end SMB platform turns it 'from AI Spender to AI winner', and 'success here will likely come AT THE EXPENSE OF SHOPIFY.'** **The threat is specified rather than hand-waved, which is why it belongs on this page in detail: Meta's SMB LLM (codenamed 'Watermelon') would let a merchant PHOTOGRAPH A PRODUCT, SELECT A TARGET MARKET, and have Meta build the STOREFRONT, CATALOGUE, PRICING, CREATIVE AND CRM — then feed the data and advertising straight into Advantage+.** Redburn sizes it at **~$80bn of SMB-LLM revenue by 2030 on 30% penetration of the 250 MILLION BUSINESSES ALREADY ON META'S PLATFORM**, with the bundled stack delivering **~25% higher return on ad spend than standalone alternatives** — i.e. the displacement argument rests on an ROAS advantage Shopify structurally cannot match, not on price. **The conclusion, verbatim: 'If Meta internalizes the storefront and commerce stack FROM H2, Shopify's growth could slow MATERIALLY, and at 50x P/E, the risk/reward no longer looks attractive.'** **Two things to hold against it. First, the timing claim ('from H2') and the $80bn/2030 sizing are Redburn's model, not disclosed Meta plans — Watermelon has not been announced as a product. Second, and cutting the other way, independent corroboration arrived the SAME DAY: Meta's Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang told YC Startup School that Meta will build a HARNESS for its models plus open-source LLMs — precisely the agentic framework an SMB storefront product requires — and that 'the best AI products are yet to come'.** So the capability build is real and dated even if the commerce product is not yet announced. **The number that decides it is the 50x P/E: at that multiple the stock is priced for uninterrupted SMB share, and the debate is no longer about Shopify's execution but about whether its largest traffic source becomes its competitor.** Also noted: SHOP appeared on CNBC's biggest-analyst-calls list 07-22. Cross-filed [[META]], [[GOOG]]. |

[[AAPL]] (and to some degree [[SAMSUNG]]) appear to be WEATHERING MEMORY PRICING PRESSURE BETTER THAN CHINESE ANDROID VENDORS.'** SepQ and DecQ estimates lowered; Data Center left alone. **And the valuation mechanics are explicit rather than implied: PEG framework cut 2.4x → 1.8x, IMPLIED P/E 108x → 83x — so most of the $110 is de-rating, not earnings.** **The most useful independent corroboration this run is from an unrelated coverage name: in UBS's own [[TER]] preview, one of four reasons Teradyne's 2H will beat management's steer is that 'TER continues to hold the VAST MAJORITY OF ARM CPU TEST SHARE, WHERE UNITS HAVE BEEN UPSIDING.' A test-equipment order book is a cleaner read on actual ARM server-CPU shipments than royalty commentary — and it points the opposite way from the handset cuts, which is exactly the barbell UBS is describing.** Watch items into the print: commentary on the recent compute megaprojects (Arm/x86 CPU mix, pace of spending, any read-through on SoftBank's ARM shareholding), early shape of F2Q27, smartphone CSS design-win momentum ([[MEDIATEK]] Dimensity 9400), and general-purpose cloud compute ([[GOOG]] Axion, [[MSFT]] Cobalt, [[AMZN]] Graviton). | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ TSM | **4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (Fubon Buy NT$3,500, UBS Buy, Nomura Buy, BofA all stand). **The headline is that the EMIB-T threat finally got stress-tested by a house that covers both sides, and TSMC wins in the bull case for Intel.** UBS sizes the **advanced-packaging TAM at US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, and then hands Intel its best plausible outcome — **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030, packages for ~24mn accelerator dies, with CoPoS not reaching MP for Nvidia until 2028 and for others until 2029-30** — and TSMC *still* compounds advanced-packaging sales at a **50% CAGR 2026-30, holds 65-70% share, and takes advanced packaging to 18% of total sales in 2030E from 9% in 2026E.** In UBS's *lower* case (stronger EMIB-T adoption) that becomes **47% CAGR / 62% share / 16% of sales** — i.e. **the base-vs-bear spread is roughly 2pts of revenue mix over four years.** That is the most useful bound on packaging-disruption risk this page has, and it argues the debate is worth far less to TSMC's numbers than to Intel's. **Not smoothed over: UBS also says EMIB-T reaches MP in H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028, and names TSMC's own three execution risks — "CoPoS warpage, panel process uniformity, and new equipment readiness."** The compensating technical case: CoPoS offers "tighter interconnect, better signal integrity, broader integration options such as 3D stacking (and eventually, CPO)"; EMIB-T's edge is structural cost (no interposer) and scalability to ultra-large packages on large substrates. UBS's own framing of the outcome — **EMIB-T as "a meaningful second-source for selected hyperscaler ASICs" while CoPoS stays "the preferred platform for customers prioritizing the highest performance and execution reliability"** — is a share-of-mind concession, not a share-of-revenue one. **Second, and it corroborates the above from a completely different seat: an ex-ASM International senior process engineer (AlphaSense expert call, 07-25) independently concludes that TSMC's dominance holds and that the binding constraint is TOOL availability — specifically bonding tools — "not… financial or market factors,"** with demand for CoWoS and CoPoS "outpacing supply for the foreseeable future," the market **bifurcated (TSMC in complex, OSATs in mature)**, and the TSMC-NVIDIA relationship "deeply integrated… making it difficult for competitors to break in." Caveat kept on the page: it is a 2-page expert-network summary and the expert's background is front-end ALD/process integration, so it is a well-informed prior rather than a supply-chain audit. **Third — a pattern worth naming rather than a one-off: on glass-core substrates TSMC is ALSO behind Intel.** MS's checks have **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden reaching MP only in 2H28 or 2029, versus Intel/Lens Tech with a pilot line by end-2026 and meaningful volume from 2H27** — the same ~12-18-month ordering as EMIB-T vs CoPoS. On both bridges and substrates, Intel is earlier and TSMC is betting on the higher-performance version arriving later. Glass core is directly relevant because it attacks **warpage**, one of the three CoPoS risks UBS names. **Fourth, restated because it is management's own words and was not on this page in this form: "TSMC highlighted at its 2Q26 call that back-end sits in deeper shortage than front-end, with packaging capacity so tight it now limits customer growth"** (via Jefferies) — i.e. packaging, not wafers, is the named growth constraint, which is the demand-side justification for the entire capex build above, plus **two ADDITIONAL advanced-packaging fabs announced on the call** (a positive read to [[KLAC]]'s Advanced Packaging SPC line, C26 forecast $1B / +57% y/y). Minor housekeeping note: BofA's weekend desk recap carried TSMC 2026 capex as "$58B" in a one-line summary while its own semicap section used **$60-64B from $52-56B** — the **$60-64B guide already confirmed on this page (07-16 call) stands**; the $58B is a modelled/actual figure, not the guide. Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[NVDA]], [[KLAC]], [[MEDIATEK]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/optical-cpo](themes/optical-cpo.md). |

Nothing superseded.** **(1) The China-supply question got its first proper sell-side anchor: Nomura initiated CXMT (688825 CH) at Buy, TP CNY116, forecasting its global DRAM share going ~10% → ~18% by end-2028F on 40-45% bit-CAGR expansion — which independently corroborates the 17%-of-DRAM-supply-by-YE28 figure Susquehanna put on this page on 07-25, from a house that is long the stock.** Two bears agreeing is weak evidence; a bull and a bear agreeing on the supply number is much stronger, and that is the reason to carry it. CXMT **listed in Shanghai 7/27** at a debut valuation BofA's desk framed as **15-20x FY27 P/E → ~RMB5T (bull 30x → ~RMB9T)** — a multiple of SK hynix's own market cap on roughly 10% share, i.e. **the threat is cost of capital, not technology.** The gate is export control (**MATCH Act**; CXMT equipment localisation ~40%; worst case its **Shanghai capacity — 30-40% of total by 2028F — pushed out**). **(2) The same note contains the strongest structural demand statement now on this page, and it also comes from the competitor's analyst: global memory usage up more than SEVENFOLD over 2026-30F (60%+ bit CAGR) "even after accounting for potential adoption of memory-efficiency technologies (discounting memory usage by 4x)," against industry bit expansion of only 30-40% CAGR — "that could still be insufficient."** A 4x efficiency haircut and demand still outruns supply. **(3) Positioning is genuinely contradictory across desks and is left that way, because the disagreement is the information: MS Prime has HFs recovering ~50% of the capital unwound through June with the Global Memory (MSXXGMEM) L/S ratio at ~7x vs peaks >20x — i.e. re-engaged but nowhere near crowded — and MS's EU client poll has 61% saying now is the time to add to memory longs (Semis the preferred 2H26 TMT sector at 57%). And Jefferies' own desk flow says "memory our most sold sector" on Friday.** The likely reconciliation is the lookback window (MS is comparing to 2026 peaks, JPM to history) but it is not reconciled here. Against all of it, memory was the best-performing sub-sector on the tape last week (**MU +8%, WDC +9%, STX +8%, SNDK +6%**) while the **SOX sat -19% off its June high** — the pricing checks (**+15-20% m/m July PC DRAM contract, implying +30-40% q/q vs TrendForce's +13-18%**) are being paid for even as the sector derates. | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ SAMSUNG | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch into the 7/29 print). 5 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** — but the page now carries what is, on its face, **the most consequential single datapoint of the quarter: Samsung announced a $200bn / 5-year chip supply contract with [[AVGO]] covering memory, foundry AND advanced packaging** (Reuters, 2026-07-25: "Samsung Elec wins $200 billion Broadcom AI chip partnership, boosting foundry push"). It matters for three reasons, in order: **(1) it is a foundry win**, and the standing bear on this page has been precisely that Samsung Foundry cannot land a marquee leading-edge logic customer at scale — Broadcom is the largest merchant custom-ASIC house there is; **(3) ~$40bn/yr against Samsung's current foundry run-rate is transformational and, if it converts, forces the capex line materially higher** — BofA's desk drew that inference the same day ("bodes well for Samsung capex"), which makes it a WFE read-through as well ([[KLAC]] / [[LRCX]] / [[AMAT]] / [[ASML]], filed to [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md)). **Deliberately NOT treated as a booked number:** as reported it is a supply-contract/partnership *value*, not backlog; no node, product or phasing split has been disclosed; and BofA's own weekend note hedged it as "AVGO **possibly** doing $200B deal with Samsung foundry," i.e. the desk was reading the same press we are. **Management gets its first chance to size or qualify it on 7/29 — that is the thing to watch, not the headline.** One cross-read worth holding: Hock Tan told JPM on 07-07 that Broadcom keeps majority TPU volume with Google, so this plausibly attaches to non-Google ASIC programs and/or HBM/packaging supply rather than implying a Google-share shift. **Second thread — the AMD content position is now explicitly two-sided and both sides stay on the page:** Jefferies reports Samsung "currently supplies HBM4 for AMD's MI455 Instinct GPUs and serves as the dedicated supplier of DDR5 solutions for the 6th-generation EPYC CPUs deployed in the Helios platform," while SemiAnalysis (07-21) documented a quantified HBM-content cut on a Meta-specific cut-down MI part and (07-24) that MSFT "dropped AMD after MI300X due to unreliable Samsung 2023 HBM memory." Named flagship supplier and carrier of the quality stigma on the same account — the **dedicated DDR5 sole-source language on 6th-gen EPYC is the cleaner win**. **Third: an [[AAPL]] System-LSI content gain — Samsung (with Novatek) takes iPhone 18 display-driver-IC share from LX Semicon, whose iPhone DDI share goes from 50%± (2022-25) to ~10%± on iPhone 18 (so ~90%+ to Novatek/Samsung), on Pro / Pro Max / foldable**, and for a technical reason (iPhone 18 "requires more advanced power/heating control") rather than price — which makes it more durable (BofA/Simon Woo, 07-26; LXS downgraded to Underperform, PO W63,000 → W25,000). Also logged: **South Korea's ~$950bn of new AI initiatives** announced around President Lee's US tour (Reuters, 07-25), and BofA reiterating both its **memory-bull stance into "a big week for memory — CXMT IPO, Kioxia, Samsung"** and its expectation of a **massive buyback + earlier dividends at the 7/29 call** (already on page from 07-24). | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ NVDA | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 6 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (MS Top Pick $288, BofA $320/$350, Barclays OW $275, Cantor all stand). **Two items dominate and they point in opposite directions, which is the honest state of the name.** **(1) THE BACKSTOP GOES TO $250BN — reported, not announced. WSJ says Nvidia is in talks to provide a $250bn backstop for [[OPENAI]] to help it lease a 10GW Ohio data-center project from SoftBank, with the project costing ~$500bn including the chips.** This is the **exact mechanism already documented on this page from SemiAnalysis's "AI Project Trinity" (07-06), scaled to one counterparty and one site, at roughly an order of magnitude above anything previously disclosed** — NVDA guarantees the lease, SoftBank owns the asset, OpenAI takes the capacity, NVDA books the chips. **Status is talks per WSJ; not confirmed by NVDA, OpenAI or SoftBank, and NOT carried here as a commitment.** JPM's framing is the one worth keeping, because it inverts the usual read: this is a *test* of the tape, not a catalyst — "the sum of money in discussion is gargantuan… **provides yet another test of whether 'good news' around capex can lift semi stocks. If another $250bn guarantee isn't enough, then well… you really have to wonder what is**" — with the SOX already **-4.3% Friday and -19% off the mid-June high**. It will, in JPM's words, "invariably raise circular financing debates again." **(2) THE MEMORY BILL IS NOW VISIBLY CUTTING NVDA'S OWN SPECS, and this is the clearest margin-transfer mechanism yet logged on this page.** Jefferies' O'Mara, citing WCCFTECH: after the reported **RTX 50 ramp delay on memory costs** and the **SOCAMM spec cut**, NVDA is **now also cutting the memory spec on NVL72** — because **"memory is the majority of the BOM on VR200."** The dispersion in sell-side inputs is the striking part: rack-cost models are running on **HBM $/GB assumptions from ~$20 (Jefferies' own Sherman, which O'Mara says is too low) to $53/GB in 2027 — a ~2.7x spread** — with one broker at **~$9.1m per rack**. O'Mara's conclusion: "Nvidia has a memory problem in its BOM and earnings structure… trying to address this by reducing the specs across multiple products." Trade-press + desk opinion, not NVDA disclosure — but it is the first *observable* evidence that the memory upcycle this book is long elsewhere ([[MU]], [[SKHYNIX]], [[SNDK]]) is being paid for partly out of NVDA's bill of materials. **(3) A clean policy split now sits between the compute layer and the model layer: NVDA published an open letter urging Washington NOT to impose sweeping restrictions on open-weight models (with MSFT co-leading, post-Kimi K3), while [[OPENAI]] and [[ANTHROPIC]] are reported to be lobbying FOR restrictions** ("increasingly rattled by China's advances"; the administration leaning toward per-model national-security review rather than a blanket ban — NYT). Consistent with UBS's read that at the GPU layer open-model traction reallocates rather than reduces demand and is **"bullish Nvidia."** **(4) Two expert calls with hard engineering numbers: next-gen GPUs reaching up to 2,400W TDP with D2C liquid cooling the current standard and advanced alternatives (two-phase immersion, TEC, new TIMs) "two to three years away" from commercial deployment; and the cost governor that cooling "should not exceed 9-10% of total data center operating or CapEx costs."** Separately, an MSFT data-center program manager puts **solid-state transformers as the eventual 800V DC standard but "not… widespread adoption before 2030-2032"** — ⚠️ **materially later than the vendor timeline on [[GEV]]'s page** (5MW indoor SST shipping to a hyperscaler this year, 6MW outdoor over 18 months, revenue "'27 and beyond"). Logged as a vendor-vs-buyer gap, not resolved. **(5) New non-tech verticals: BMY deploying Vera Rubin racks and a 5-year LLY/NVDA agreement** — pharma is new to this page — plus real-world token evidence from **AT&T at ~1tn+ tokens/month / ~16.4tn annualized, >100 GenAI models in production (~40% on AMD hardware)**. Also: **Feynman GPU + Rosa CPU targeting TSMC A16 around 2028** (Commercial Times, trade press). BofA's **NVDA buy-side bogey poll is open into the 8/26 AMC print**. Cross-filed [[OPENAI]], [[MU]], [[TSM]], [[AMD]], [[GEV]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md), [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ MU | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (BofA Buy $1,550, TD Cowen Buy $1,600, Mizuho, Susquehanna, MS all stand). **The competitive side of this page changed materially this weekend, and it did so through a bull note on the competitor rather than a bear note on MU. Nomura initiated coverage of ChangXin Memory (CXMT, 688825 CH) at Buy, TP CNY116 — and benchmarked the multiple explicitly off Micron: CXMT should trade at "2x the valuation of its major US competitor, Micron… which has traded at ~10x over the past five years."** The forecast that matters here: **CXMT's global DRAM share going from ~10% today to ~18% by end-2028F on 40-45% bit-CAGR expansion 2026-30F.** That **independently corroborates Susquehanna's 17%-of-DRAM-supply-by-YE28 estimate already on this page (07-25)** — and it does so from a house that is long the stock, which makes the agreement more informative than two bears converging. **CXMT listed in Shanghai on 7/27**, with BofA's APAC desk reporting a **base-case valuation of 15-20x FY27 P/E → ~RMB5T market cap and a bull case of 30x → ~RMB9T** (roughly US$700bn-1.3tn on ~10% DRAM share) — the cleanest illustration on this page of the **cost-of-capital asymmetry MU faces against a state-supported competitor**, and the reason the ASP threat is not simply a function of CXMT's technology. **Both sides of the CXMT question are kept, because they are different arguments and both are true: it is a commodity-DRAM ASP threat, and per BofA/Arya it "remains largely confined to commodity DRAM and is not a credible near-term competitor in HBM3E/HBM4 AI memory."** **Second: Micron is now publicly on the record against an Apple lobbying push — WSJ reports Apple lobbying the White House "aggressively for permission to utilize Chinese memory chips in devices sold outside the US" with MU "fiercely opposed," warning China's makers "could decimate the American memory industry over time just as occurred in steel and manufacturing."** Escalations vs the 06-25 story on file: the ask is now a specific and much more grantable **ex-US carve-out**, and MU's opposition carries an explicit industrial-policy frame. The 06-29 KIS rebuttal still stands as the counter (China restricts foreign supply anyway; CXMT shifted LPDDR→DDR for domestic DCs) — so this changes no supply yet, but it does establish that customer pressure has reached the White House, in the same week as the CXMT initiation and IPO. **Third, the bullish half of Nomura's own work is the strongest demand statement now on this page and it comes from the competitor's analyst: global memory usage rising more than SEVENFOLD over 2026-30F (60%+ bit CAGR) "even after accounting for potential adoption of memory-efficiency technologies (discounting memory usage by 4x)," against all memory makers expanding at only a 30-40% bit CAGR — "that could still be insufficient."** A 4x efficiency haircut still leaves demand outrunning supply by a wide margin; that is the tightest formulation of the structural bull case on file. **Fourth, restated with the consensus gap explicit: BofA/Simon Woo's July checks have Tier-2 OEMs and module makers paying 15-20% higher PC DRAM contract prices m/m, implying +30-40% q/q against TrendForce's +13-18% for 3Q ex-HBM** — and the tape is paying for it, with **MU +8% last week (STX +8%, WDC +9%, SNDK +6%) while the SOX sat -19% off its high.** Also net-new from Arya's defence: **Kimi K3 requires ~1.4TB of HBM per serving instance despite ultra-low API pricing**, and open-weight models "multiply memory demand because every customer deployment creates a new memory endpoint" — the Jevons argument stated as architecture rather than elasticity. ⚠️ One data-integrity note: Nomura's quoted **closing price of CNY8.66 against a CNY116 TP** cannot be reconciled with a 20x-2028F-EPS-of-CNY5.8 derivation; the stock listed 7/27, so the close is pre-listing/stale and only the TP is usable. Cross-filed [[AAPL]], [[SAMSUNG]], [[SKHYNIX]], [[SNDK]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md), [themes/china-export](themes/china-export.md). |

Also added: **UBS states "we like MediaTek on its TPU opportunity and see the EMIB-T execution risks as manageable,"** with its own checks putting **EMIB-T validation yields at ~90-92% and EMIB-T substrate yields possibly ~50% (vs >80% for HPC ABF), but Ibiden capacity expansion "could offset lower yields and support selected products like Google's TPU v9"** — a direct rebuttal to Fubon's "EMIB-T not yet MP-ready" bear, and the reason MS wants the CoWoS-backup question asked. New competitive vector logged: **Qualcomm entering ASIC design services.** Edits: 3 Debate bullets, 2 Catalysts bullets, this entry. | 2026-07-26 | ⭐ INTC | **/wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). 4 intra-quarter rows. No rating, PT or estimate superseded** (UBS Neutral $121, MS EW $84, JPM UW $85, Barclays EW $100, BofA Buy $160, Arete Neutral $99 all stand). **The material item is UBS's first full-length EMIB-T-vs-CoPoS deep dive, and its contribution is to make the yield picture on this page WORSE-defined, not better — which is the honest outcome and is recorded as such.** UBS says Intel "must lift **EMIB-T packaging from ~90-92% validation yields** toward high-volume manufacturing levels," and that **EMIB-T substrate fabrication yields "may currently be at ~50%, vs >80% for HPC ABF substrates."** Set that against the marks already on the page — **Fubon 60-70% (06-05), KeyBanc "reached 98%" (07-24), ex-Intel CVP Kaizad "mature yield 98-99%" (07-16)** — and the **packaging-yield estimates now span 60% to 99%, while substrate estimates span ~50% (UBS) to 60-70% (KeyBanc)**. Two things make this tractable rather than noise: UBS's figure is the **only one explicitly labelled *validation* yield** (which may reconcile part of the spread against production-yield marks), and UBS's own base case **routes around the substrate problem rather than solving it — "capacity expansion at Ibiden could offset lower yields and support selected products like Google's TPU v9."** So the constraint identified on 07-25 (substrates, not bridges) survives, with a lower number attached and a named workaround. **Not resolved. The 95%+ Google TPU-V9 gate remains the single item on which the Foundry thesis turns, and this page now carries three incompatible readings of it.** Net-new sizing: **advanced-packaging TAM US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR**, with UBS's *bullish* Intel stress test at **13% industry share / US$16bn of sales by 2030 (~24mn accelerator dies)** — a case in which [[TSM]] still compounds advanced-packaging sales at 50% and holds 65-70% share. **EMIB-T's structural advantages, per UBS: interposer elimination (structural cost), and scalability to ultra-large packages by building directly on large substrates — with MP targeted H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028.** And the two Intel-specific risks UBS names that were not on this page: **"integration between the foundry (TSMC) and back-end (Intel) processes, as well as yield responsibility"** — a contracting problem (who eats a yield loss when TSMC owns the die and Intel owns the package), not a technical one, and arguably the harder of the two to fix. Rating stays Neutral **explicitly on valuation**, not on execution. **Second net-new thread — glass core substrates now have a dated Intel ramp: Lens Tech (300433.SZ) is partnering with Intel on glass core with TGVs, pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27"** (MS/Derrick Yang), possibly with Korean players too. That is the structural answer to the substrate-yield constraint (lower signal loss, CTE matching against warpage, mechanical strength at panel scale), and on MS's checks Intel is **~12-18 months ahead of the TSMC/Ibiden glass path (Innolux MP 2H28-2029; BOE capacity 2027 for 2028 MP)** — the same ordering UBS gives for EMIB-T vs CoPoS. MS's caveat kept: helps sentiment, "more meaningful revenue contribution should take time," hurdles are "process control instead of pure equipment capability." **Third: 14A described as ahead of where 18A was — "defect density and transistor performance are outpacing 18A development"** (Jefferies), with Intel "aggressively locking in tool purchase orders and accelerating cleanroom buildouts," 14A "likely to be a material user of high-NA," and [[ASML]] revenue from Intel "subdued this year but likely to accelerate in 2027." The gate is unchanged and worth repeating: **the WFE step-up requires a named 14A customer, and official C27 guidance is ~6 months out.** **Fourth, the tape, recorded because it is the actual outcome: INTC closed -8% Friday having been +5% pre-market on results JPM's Harlan Sur said "cleared expectations by wide margins" — the SOX is -19% from its mid-June high, CPUs have derated -21% since June 22, and INTC is -34% since the Tech-Momentum ATH.** JPM's positioning work explains part of it — aggregate equity positioning light (US TPM 19th %ile) but **global semis crowded at the 98th %ile on both net exposure and L/S ratios**. Cross-filed [[TSM]], [[ASML]], [[MEDIATEK]], [[GOOG]], [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md), [themes/semicap-wfe](themes/semicap-wfe.md). |

| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ GLW | (/wiki-ingest — Outlook, weekend batch). 1 intra-quarter row. Nothing superseded.** **A new glass-intensive semiconductor end market is forming and the named participants are Asian display-chain incumbents, not Corning — logged as an open competitive question rather than an assumed win.** MS (Derrick Yang): **Lens Tech announced a partnership with Intel on glass core substrates with TGVs — pilot line ready by end-2026 and "more meaningful volume shipments might start from 2H27,"** possibly with Korean partners too; **Innolux + TSMC + Ibiden** at MP 2H28 or 2029; **BOE** with capacity in 2027 for 2028 MP, "aspiration to finish the whole glass core ABF substrate process"; **AUO** on glass for LEO antenna modules and micro-LED optical. The design-in rationale is squarely a materials argument — glass offers **"cost advantages from larger dimensions… better electrical performance, leading to lower signal loss, less mismatch of coefficients of thermal expansion… to mitigate the warpage issue, and greater mechanical strength"** — and **the value capture hinges on process control, not equipment: "the requirement for precision and density is much higher than what they used to deal with… if they can continue to improve the yield, we believe they will likely take a relatively big portion of the value added in the mass production stage."** The reason to track it here: **glass core is the structural answer to the ~50% ABF-substrate-yield constraint on Intel's EMIB-T and to CoPoS warpage at TSMC** (see [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md)), so the TAM is real and growing with advanced packaging — the question is whether Corning is in it. GLW reports **7/28 BMO.** Cross-filed [[INTC]], [[TSM]]. |
| 2026-07-26 | ⭐ GEV | /wiki-ingest (Outlook, weekend batch). **4 intra-quarter rows. Nothing superseded** — but four call details are net-new and two of them directly address the fear the stock sold off on. **(1) Equipment pricing is now quantified: "1H26 orders were priced more than 20% above 4Q25 orders," with 2H gas equipment orders expected at a $/kW "at the higher end of the range of 10 to 20 points versus 4Q'25."** First hard price-realisation number on this page, against a dated base — the margin guide is being driven by price, not just volume. **(2) The 30 GW capacity target finally has its baseline, and it changes the read: it is a raise from a 24 GW FY28 plan, "through LEAN and add'l machinery."** The stock fell **-9%** on the print partly because a new ~30 GW capacity target revived overcapacity fears; a **+25% productivity raise off an existing 24 GW plan is a very different thing from a greenfield capital commitment**, and the page previously carried only the 30 GW endpoint. **(3) The forward book runs further than the page had it: "over half of '31 GWs will be on contract by the end of the year with active discussions for 2032 and beyond,"** and on peak-orders — **"continue to see very healthy growth above 125 gigawatts in '27 relative to year-end '26… confident in continuing to grow the contracted backlog for the better part of the next six quarters… even with the higher output."** **(4) Segment guidance for 2026: Power revenue +18-20% at 17-19% EBITDA margins; Electrification $14.5-15B revenue at 18-20% EBITDA margins; Wind revenue down LDD with ~$400mm of EBITDA losses.** Also logged: **~100 customers in 26 countries, 80% traditional / 20% data centers**, agreements into 2031, international pipeline (Taiwan, Saudi, Mexico, SE Asia, Qatar), and aeroderivatives explicitly framed as a **bridge** to heavy-duty rather than a substitute. **⚠️ One genuine cross-source gap logged rather than resolved — the SST adoption curve.** Management now describes **two distinct hyperscaler programs (a 5MW indoor unit shipping later this year for testing, and a 6MW outdoor unit with a different hyperscaler over ~18 months), both R&D-shared, with revenue "'27 and beyond."** But a **Microsoft data-centre program manager (AlphaSense expert call, 07-26, filed on [[NVDA]]) believes SST "will eventually become the industry standard for 800V DC" but "does not expect widespread adoption before 2030-2032,"** with 800V still "limited to pilot deployments" and most data centres on AC. GEV is selling into 2027; the buyer is planning for 2030-2032. Both can be true (pilots '27, standard '30-'32) but the implied revenue slopes differ a lot. Filed to [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). **Finally, a larger US supply-gap frame from BofA's oil-and-gas team: "US electricity demand requires the equivalent of 177GW of new capacity between 2026-30, but utilities are likely to supply just 93 gigawatts, leaving a gap exceeding 80GW,"** with **"hyperscalers plan[ning] to spend over $5trn in the next five years, yet only ~9% of US data centers have onsite generation"** and an 84GW opportunity if all behind-the-meter demand were met by gas. Note the source's own interest — the note is a Buy case for CVX/WMB/KGS/PUMP/LBRT as onsite-generation suppliers, i.e. partly substitution *away* from grid-scale CCGT — but the 177-vs-93GW gap is larger than the ">100GW shortfall" this page already carries from Obin's 07-08 "Power gap." Cross-filed [[NVDA]], [[CEG]], [[TLN]], [themes/ai-datacenter-power](themes/ai-datacenter-power.md), [themes/800v-dc-power](themes/800v-dc-power.md). |

Directionally consistent with the Jefferies/Fubon 12-15M-TPU-chips-in-2028 figure already on the page. Also flagged as a new competitive vector: **Qualcomm entering ASIC design services**. No rating or PT superseded** (BofA Buy $380, UBS Neutral $296, MS OW $364, KeyBanc UW all stand). **The single most consequential item is a policy escalation, not an estimate: WSJ reports Apple is "lobbying the White House aggressively for permission to utilize Chinese memory chips in devices sold outside the US," with [[MU]] "fiercely opposed," warning China's memory makers "could decimate the American memory industry over time just as occurred in steel and manufacturing."** Two things are net-new against the 06-25 CXMT-lobbying story and the 06-29 KIS rebuttal already on this page: the **ex-US carve-out** (a much narrower and therefore more grantable ask than "use Chinese DRAM") and **Micron's named public opposition with an explicit industrial-policy frame**. It does *not* resolve the KIS objection on file (China would restrict foreign supply; CXMT has shifted LPDDR→DDR for domestic datacentres), so the relief stays hypothetical — but Apple escalating to the White House is the best available measure of how binding the memory bill has become, and it lands the same weekend Nomura initiated **CXMT at Buy** with global DRAM share going ~10% → ~18% by end-2028F. **Second: a genuine sell-side-vs-buy-side gap on the September guide, logged rather than reconciled — BofA/Wamsi models F4Q revenue $106B and EPS $1.88 against Street $114B/$2.01, while the buy-side bogey for the Q4 revenue *guide* is $116.0bn.** Wamsi's own number therefore sits ~$10bn below the bogey; his defence is that the compression is transitory and pre-paid — **product margins −190bps q/q to 36.8% (June), −280bps q/q to 34.1% (September), then recovering to 38.5% in December on higher launch pricing plus a $3B tariff benefit** — which is the direct counter to UBS's 07-25 BOM arithmetic. **Third, and the reason to keep both sides: UBS's Vogt has moved. He "found himself more constructive 3 months ago" and is now "increasingly less positive… given a mid-30s P/E, 60% premium to the S&P underpinned by ~10% EPS growth"** — a valuation objection that does not depend on unit or memory assumptions at all, and the first of its kind on this page. **Fourth, three supply-chain reads that are more informative than the previews:** (a) **Counterpoint has Q2'26 global PC shipments −4% to 65m while Apple GREW 13%** (Asus +4%, Lenovo −2%, Dell −6%, HP −8%) — third-party quantification of the Mac share gain UBS flagged qualitatively; (b) **BofA/Simon Woo downgraded LX Semicon to Underperform (PO W63,000 → W25,000) because iPhone 18 DDI orders moved to Novatek and Samsung — LXS's iPhone display-DDI share going from 50%± (2022-25) to ~10%± — explicitly because iPhone 18 "requires more advanced power/heating control"**, i.e. a component-level tell that iPhone 18 is a real thermal/power redesign, and a [[SAMSUNG]] content gain; (c) **Jefferies attributes part of STM's H2-26 shortfall to "a material YoY fall in its Apple-related sales… due to the lack of Face ID in the foldable iPhone"** — a product-spec inference from a supplier's guide, flagged for confirmation at launch rather than treated as settled. Positioning: AAPL near all-time highs while Mag7 posted its worst relative week in ~4 years, **14D RSI 65 (3rd most overbought in Jefferies' Tech universe)**, MS clients have it "back in safe-haven mode ahead of the iPhone 18 launch," and BofA calls it "the other side of the capex trade via memory + continued FCF strength." Also carried: **consensus now models a 12% iPhone revenue CAGR FY25-28 vs 6.5% at the start of the AI cycle — ~$100bn of incremental iPhone revenue over three years.** Cross-filed [[MU]], [[SAMSUNG]], [[DELL]], [themes/hbm-memory](themes/hbm-memory.md). |

Logged as a competitor-note read-through, not a GEV call. **(2) BofA cross-sector power team + Ross Fowler's NARUC 2026 takeaways** — following the prior week's **100GW gap between power demand and supply through 2030**, the deeper cut on behind-the-meter (the "only option," since in-front-of-the-meter is spoken for and the grid is maxxed out) finds **158GW of announced BTM projects but only 30-50% expected to get through — a residual >50GW gap**, with **Chevron and Williams the likely main deliverers**. From NARUC, Fowler reports regulators shifting from "demand only" to "**show me they're real**," with **speed-to-market now the critical bottleneck** and execution mattering more than opportunity. Logged as a new monetisable layer of CoWoS-L value-add and as a raised technical bar for Intel EMIB-T (which SemiAnalysis separately judges behind partly because TSMC is "further along on… active LSI"). Also: **MI500 (2027) pivots to co-packaged copper + near-packaged optics** (9 optical engines × 6.4 Tbit/s uni-di = 57.6T scale-up, light from ELSFPs) — the AMD-side analogue of the COUPE/CPO inflection already tracked here. **(2) BACKFILL — SemiAnalysis "EDA Market Primer" (2026-05-21)** and **(3) BACKFILL — SemiAnalysis/STEEL "Is SMIC N+3's Metal Pitch Smaller than Intel 18A's?" (2026-06-14)**, both folded as **dated structural bullets in Debate/thesis, NOT as intra-quarter rows** — their dates fall inside the Q1 FY26 window which was **CLOSED by the 2Q26 print (07-16)**, so per the run's backfill convention they are logged as structural context rather than injected into a closed log. From the EDA primer: TSMC as **customer and gatekeeper** (Calibre-clean + PrimeTime mandated at 7/5/3nm, PDKs co-developed ~24 months pre-production), **Taiwan = 17% of Big-3 EDA revenue from <2% of global GDP, "entirely a TSMC ecosystem effect"**, the OIP Silicon-IP library 3,000 (2010) → **93,000 items (2025)**, **Fab 18 from 4 customers/8 tape-outs (2020) → 45/262 (2024)**, TSMC never built competitive internal EDA (Intel/Samsung tried and abandoned), Cadence's expanded TSMC N2/A16 collaboration, and the outside-view risk line *"ASML faces High-NA adoption risk, and TSMC faces geopolitical concentration"* — an independent restatement of the Taiwan-concentration bear already on the page (Barron's "80% of long-term assets still in Taiwan"). From the SMIC teardown: **TSMC N6 is the measured baseline** and **SMIC N+3 "reaches TSMC N6-class logic density… [but] does not match N6 on process maturity or cost"**; N6 fin pitch measured **34nm** with density from **DTCO not tighter pitches**, HD cell 240nm, no COAG, M0/M2/M3 ~40nm on SADP with **no quadruple patterning needed anywhere** (the cost/maturity moat, quantified); TSMC used the reduced p-to-n isolation booster on N3; a theoretical SMIC N+4 reaches only N5-class density; and the historical note that **Huawei was TSMC's largest customer pre-controls — the sole customer on N7+, TSMC's first EUV node, and among the first on N5 alongside Apple — ending in late 2020**. Against MS base-case **FY28 $157bn revenue** (incl. **$16.5/Watt on avg 6 GW** and **35mn avg consumer subs @ $46/mo ARPU**) **and $69bn EBIT**: **$100 = 18x EV/EBIT FY28** (≈ comp median but on **3x the revenue CAGR, 76% vs 26% for 2025-30**) and **16x FY28 P/E — a >50% discount to WMT (33x) and 40% to TJX (26.5x)**. Risks Jonas concedes: **lockup releases ("supply is undefeated?")** and a print that "may leave many key investor questions unanswered." Also logged: **"SpaceX has erased $1 trillion in market value over the past month"** (@Hedgeye, 07-22); Musk on the TSLA call — "sees more overlap between Tesla, SpaceX… can't comment on potential merger" (@zerohedge, 07-22), and a related Jonas piece **"Starlink and Tesla… Connective Tissue for Robot Swarm"** (07-24); and the [[MEDIATEK]] cross-read that a **second hyperscaler ASIC project in final design is speculated to be xAI/SpaceX** (Jefferies sales, 07-23) — unmodelled on both pages. | 2026-07-24 | ⭐ MU | (/wiki-ingest — 4 sources; additive, no PT/rating/estimate superseded).** **MS · Joseph Moore via MS TMT sales (Wigg, 07-24) — the defence of the dip:** after fresh checks with **data-center purchasing contacts**, MS **defended MU and SNDK and called the memory selloff "a compelling entry point."** Core claim: **memory is increasingly THE bottleneck to AI builds (and agentic-CPU builds), durably so** — "there isn't enough memory vs AI requirements, and he just doesn't see that changing," with the shortage expected to **intensify in 2027 and again in 2028**. Specifics: **data-center memory prices +25%+ in 3Q** (deceleration vs 2Q's larger increases, "but that has been obvious"); **NAND checks inflecting recently**. **The framing worth carrying into the cycle debate on this page: "LTAs and de-speccing could flatten the amplitude of the cycle vs inflated expectations — but increase duration, which is likely better for stocks in the long run"** — i.e. the SCA/take-or-pay structure is an **amplitude-for-duration trade**, which is the mechanism behind (not a contradiction of) Arete's ~30x-P/E re-rating argument already on the page. **BofA "Global Memory Tech" (Woo/Shen/Arya, 07-23/24)** corroborates on pricing: **PC DRAM July contract prices +15-20% m/m at more Tier-2 OEMs/module makers, implying +30-40% q/q vs consensus +13-18%** (TrendForce, ex-HBM); spot +2-3% w/w; **China memory imports at a record US$32bn (+~250% y/y) despite rising domestic output — "CXMT's expanding capacity has had only a limited impact."** **The bear/tape side, logged as mechanism not just price:** Asian memory was very weak on 07-23 with the **KOSPI -5.7% on no memory-specific newsflow** (Rothschild · Adley, 07-24), possibly a read-across from the US session "or even the Anthropic datapoint" (his unverified TickerTrends read that Anthropic ARR growth has slowed — see [[ANTHROPIC]]); and **@jukan05's 07-23 report that YMTC has surpassed Micron and SanDisk in NAND share (gap to Kioxia now 0.4pp)** triggered selling he then pushed back on: *"**NAND is still in short supply, and that shortage will persist. NVIDIA's CMX racks are currently so constrained by NAND shortages that they cannot even be shipped fully populated.**"* **Both sides stay on the page: YMTC share risk is real; the physical NAND tightness proof point is also real** — and the week's drawdown was driven by share-shift headlines and frontier-lab ARR perception at a moment when the pricing checks were beating. Also logged: **BofA's Arya arguing open-source AI is *bullish* for memory** — closed models amortise demand across shared HBM pools in a few data centres, whereas **open models replicate weights into a separate HBM pool with every self-hosted deployment** (via @jukan05, 07-22); and investors pointing to **CXMT's Monday debut as a supply-risk marker**, against Reuters reporting **CXMT has been repeatedly *raising* prices to Huawei**. | 2026-07-24 | ⭐ MSFT | /wiki-ingest — 2 sources into the FQ4 print; additive, no rating/PT superseded (MS OW $600 retained).** **MS · Adam Wood / Josh Baer / Jonathan Eisenson "4Q26 Preview – Time to Take Back the Narrative" (07-23)** is the most detailed preview now on the page, and the value is in the **explicit bogeys**: (1) **Azure ~41% cc in Q4 — a 1pt beat vs the 39-40% cc guide high end** — against a 4pt tougher comp, with consensus at **40.5% as-reported / ~$3.3bn of q/q dollars added**; (2) a **reiterated F1H27 "modest acceleration," i.e. 42%+ cc**; (3) **6mn+ Copilot seat net adds** on top of **20mn paid seats**. MS models **Azure AI ~100% y/y (~18% q/q, ~$1,260mn q/q dollars added, ~19pts of contribution)**. **The framing worth carrying forward:** CFO Amy Hood has said Azure guidance is an **"allocated capacity guide"** — reported growth is governed by **datacenter buildout and GPU-deployment timing becoming revenue-additive, not by demand** — and incremental capacity must be split across **external Azure, first-party AI apps, and internal R&D/training**, which structurally caps the size of quarterly upside surprises. Baseline from last quarter: Azure **+39% cc** (above the ~38% high end), **~160bps y/y op-margin expansion**, **RPO +110% y/y to ~$625bn**, **cRPO +39% y/y**. Copilot reframed as a **three-pronged ARPU story — seats, the E7 SKU, and consumption pricing** ("one of the largest ARPU expansion opportunities in MSFT history"). Channel checks constructive across Azure/M365/Security, with **one partner flagging capacity constraints limiting deployment pace** (consistent with management) and **Copilot adoption "progressing gradually, with awareness continuing to outpace enterprise-wide deployment."** **MS 2Q26 CIO survey:** software spend accelerating to **+4.1% in 2026** (from +3.8%); MSFT the **highest forward spend intentions at +7.6%** (from +7.3%); **72% of CIOs increasing MSFT spend**; Azure the largest expected cloud share gainer over both 1yr and 3yr; MSFT the largest expected beneficiary of incremental GenAI spend and preferred platform for agentic automation and custom AI apps. **Two-sided item logged rather than smoothed over:** the same survey shows **both MSFT and AMZN ceding budget share to LLM providers** on 1-year and medium-term views — MSFT wins the vendor race while the category leaks to the model layer. On capex: **"Consensus capex estimates could move higher; we see less upside to our higher-than-street capex estimates,"** with **capex-growth moderation named as important for ROIC and the shares**, against a plan to **roughly double the datacenter footprint over two years**. Valuation: **~16x FY28 GAAP EPS**, and a 1.2x PEG / 25x PE (a discount to peers) supports **50% upside to the $600 PT**. **Counterweight added — JPM · Schilsky TECH SKETCH (07-23): "MSFT Q4 Preview – No Model, No Chip, No Hope?"** versus GOOGL where "Cloud [is] Now a TPU Story" — logged deliberately alongside the MS note because the two argue different things: MS underwrites **allocated-capacity Azure growth + Copilot ARPU**, while the bear questions **strategic position in the model and silicon layers**. The partial answer already on this page: the **07-20 Helios commitment** gives MSFT a second merchant accelerator source ([[AMD]]) and [[OPENAI]] supplies the model layer commercially. Edits: 2 new Intra-quarter Full-log rows (both 07-23). |

this Sept-2025 note **pre-dates that by three months and supplies the mechanism** (price-first vendor selection + direct HBM procurement + an explicit margin-compression goal), so it is filed as corroborating structural context. The 2026 bull counterweights are flagged in the same bullet — Sur (06-05/06-17) defending both roadmaps, UBS's "Celestial AI helps ensure MRVL remains an important part of the Trainium roadmap," and the Trainium-4 four-variant optionality — with the observation that none of them rebut the *margin* mechanism, only socket retention. **Light second source — SemiAnalysis "EDA Market Primer" (2026-05-21, already folded into CDNS/SNPS/themes on 07-23, not re-ingested here):** one relative-scale clause appended to the same bullet — **Alchip + GUC + Marvell Custom Silicon together ~$200-400M/yr** of all-in EDA tool + IP + emulation spend vs **Broadcom's ASIC group alone at $200-500M**. | 2026-07-24 | ⭐ ASML | (/run-inbox — 2 SemiAnalysis sources, both NET-NEW to this page; a route the keyword router missed entirely. Additive; no rating, PT, estimate or unit forecast superseded.)** Both are **read-throughs, framed as such** — neither report contains an ASML number, and both bullets carry that caveat explicitly. **(1) SemiAnalysis / STEEL Team "Is SMIC N+3's Metal Pitch Smaller than Intel 18A's?" (2026-06-14)** → one Debate/Bull bullet + an addition to the Export-control/China Catalyst + Sources. The teardown is, at root, an **EUV-denial study**, and it is the first physical measurement on this page of what the export-control regime is producing: **without EUV, SMIC leans harder on DUV immersion, SAQP multi-patterning and DTCO** — reaching **TSMC-N6-class logic density (113.4 vs 107.7 MTr/mm²)** but paying in complexity, efficiency and process control, so N+3 **"does not match N6 on process maturity or cost."** The cost is measured, not asserted: M0 at 32.5nm is "below what a single DUV-defined spacer (SADP) can resolve, so SMIC cascades a second spacer step (SAQP)," with re-entrant M0 trenches, a barrier-rich foot at the etch-stop and a **liner-bottom blowout at the alumina etch-stop** deviating from the big three — whereas **TSMC N6 reaches comparable density with M0/M2/M3 at ~40nm on SADP, needing no quadruple patterning anywhere**. The DUV-intensity conclusion is quoted verbatim on the page: *"Future nodes will be tougher… Further scaling without EUV leaves fewer levers. More aggressive multi-patterning adds masks and overlay error. SMIC can keep pushing DUV, but each step will get more expensive and less forgiving"* — i.e. **each additional node of China-domestic scaling consumes disproportionately more immersion tool-time, masks and overlay budget** (a theoretical N+4 pulls M1 to ~36nm and M2 to ~35nm, both into SAQP; a theoretical N+5 with backside contacts additionally requires new backside-alignment, wafer-thinning, contact-reveal and backside-metallisation flows). Logged as the mechanical underpinning of the page's existing China-resilience checks (FundaAI: ASML China revenue +20% in 2026, sustainable through 2027-28 on SMIC/Hua Hong quad-patterning) rather than as a new demand forecast, and tied to the **N+2/N+3 licensing to HLMC/Hua Hong at government direction** (the same DUV-intensive flow spreading to more fabs). **The counter-caveat is carried in the same bullet:** Huawei's **τ (tau) scaling / LogicFolding** routes around lithography by 3D-stacking active logic — buying density with packaging rather than patterning — and its own 2031 roadmap jump to 295 MTr/mm² per package implies "either a third active layer, **partial EUV insertion** or aggressive planar DUV scaling," so the packaging path is a partial substitute for litho intensity and even Huawei's plan does not cleanly exclude EUV. **(2) SemiAnalysis "EDA Market Primer" (Kundojjala/Patel/Wong, 2026-05-21)** → one Debate/Bear bullet + Sources: the primer's closing value-chain comparison states ASML's key structural risk in one attributed line — *"EDA is the most defensible business model in semiconductors. **ASML faces High-NA adoption risk, and TSMC faces geopolitical concentration.**"* Logged as a third-party framing of the High-NA-timing item this page already tracks under Risks and Catalysts, consistent with Bernstein's TSMC-slowest-adopter read (~2030 at A10, "too expensive") and BofA's litho-expert "TSMC a deliberate slow-adopter" call — with the page's own counter noted (High-NA is ~HSD units → 10-20 per MS 07-20, so it is an out-year multiple question, not an FY26-28 numbers question). **No intra-quarter row added for either source:** the live window is **Q2'26 (Jul 15 → )** and the Q1'26 log (Apr 15 → Jul 14) was **closed at the 2026-07-15 print**, so neither the 05-21 nor the 06-14 date is covered by an open window; both folded as dated structural context per the run's backfill convention. Cross-filed [[SMIC]], [[TSM]], [[INTC]]. |

On re-read against the page that call was too harsh in both cases — each contains a specific, quantified, ARM-first datapoint that no other source on the page carries — so both are now folded, with the reasoning stated here for the record. **(1) SemiAnalysis "EDA Market Primer" (Kundojjala/Patel/Wong, 2026-05-21)** → one Current-state bullet + one Full-log row (05-21, produto/bull). The page previously covered CSS only as a *royalty-rate* story (>10% of ASP, path to >50% of royalties). Net-new: the **licence-fee** side — **CSS licences at $10-30M vs $1-5M for individual cores** (3-10x per-deal ASP) with lock-in "into Arm's interconnect topology, memory architecture, and verification methodology," not just the ISA; the **FY2025 (Mar-2025) ~$4.0B revenue split ~55% royalty ($2.2B) / ~45% licensing ($1.8B)** with royalties "typically 1-3% of chip ASP" against Synopsys/Cadence who "almost never collect royalties"; **70%+ of new licence agreements since 2019 via Flexible Access**; and the two-sided second-order effect — **CSS shrinks the implementation-tool TAM per design while verification TAM holds or grows**, shifting customer EDA spend from synthesis/P&R toward emulation, i.e. CSS substitutes customer engineering spend rather than layering on top of it. Arm is also grouped with Rambus/Alphawave as IP firms with structurally lower per-engineer EDA spend. The prior note that "Flexible Access is already covered" was correct as far as it went — the licence-ASP and revenue-split figures were not. **(2) SemiAnalysis / STEEL "Is SMIC N+3's Metal Pitch Smaller than Intel 18A's?" (2026-06-14)** → one Debate/Bull bullet + one Full-log row (06-14, competicao/bull). Dismissed on 07-23 as "foundry-primary, incidental," but the report benchmarks Huawei's in-house TaiShan cores **directly against named Arm cores**, which makes it the best independent evidence on the page for the cost of substituting Arm IP: per clock, the **middle core ≈ Cortex-A720, tiny core ≈ Cortex-A520, prime core ≈ Cortex-X2 class (a 2021 design)**, with both smaller cores failing to hold rated max frequencies; against current IP the **Arm C1 Ultra is 45% higher per clock and 2x faster in absolute integer** (Apple M5 P-core 60% / 2.7x). SemiAnalysis credits the design work but attributes the residual gap to node access/transistor budget — a direct counterweight to the standing architecture-licence and RISC-V substitution risks in Risks. **Also edited:** the **Royalty/monetization** Signal-vs-management row (flow cell annotated with the CSS licence-ASP datapoint; signal re-evaluated from **✗ contests** to **✗ contests near-term / ⚠ nuance longer-term** — near-term royalty softness per GS/MS is unchanged, but the licence-line offset is now quantified), the quarter synthesis (new structural-backfill paragraph), and Sources. Both source dates (05-21, 06-14) fall inside the page's live intra-quarter window (May 06 → Jul 22), so rows were added rather than being confined to the Changelog. |
| 2026-07-24 | ⭐ APH | **/run-inbox — single source, an ~11-month-old BACKFILL: SemiAnalysis "H100 vs GB200 NVL72 Training Benchmarks — Power, TCO and Reliability Analysis; Software Improvement Over Time" (2025-08-20).** Nothing on this page previously referenced the GB200 NVL72 connector interface (grep confirmed: no Paladin, no Bianca, no XID mention), so this is net-new — but it is Aug-2025, so it was filed as **dated historical/structural context only**: one bullet in **Debate / thesis**, one line in **Risks**, one **Sources** entry. **Deliberately NOT added** to the Intra-quarter table (window is Q1 FY26, Apr 29 → Jun 22 2026) and **allowed to supersede nothing** — every 2026 statement on the page (JPM OW/$160, the Oberon content-step-down bear, the Luxshare/CPC China thread, the 800V power thesis, the 650 Group copper-longevity reads, Redburn's top-Buy screen) stands unchanged. **Substance:** the GB200 NVL72 copper-backplane cartridges connect to each compute tray through **Amphenol Paladin HD** male/female connectors, the female one screwed onto the **Bianca board** PCB, and "if these screws are not fastened with a precise amount of tension, the entire connector can suffer from signal integrity issues" — a direct contributor to the **XID 149** error wave SemiAnalysis traces to NVLink copper-backplane signal integrity (GB200 NVL72 **MTBI 1,000–3,000 GPU-days vs 2,000–5,000** on H100 servers; the Llama4 Maverick perf/TCO edge eroding from ~50% to 20–30% cheaper). Because the contacts are gold-plated for signal integrity they carry an endurance of **at most 200 mating cycles** before the component must be discarded (Amphenol's own Paladin HD2 datasheet, cited by SemiAnalysis), and field maintenance is swap-heavy — a **backplane swap takes 8–24 hours and requires removing 10 rack stiffeners** first, with root-causing *which* Paladin connector failed taking hours on its own. **Framed in both directions, as it should be:** a **consumable/replacement-cycle and content story** (200-cycle ceiling + swap-heavy maintenance = recurring connector demand on top of first-fit content, supporting the "more interconnect, no matter what" leg), and simultaneously a **quality/engineering-scrutiny flag** — an APH part is the assembly-tolerance-sensitive component at the centre of the most-discussed reliability problem of the Blackwell rack generation, and the concrete mechanism behind the standing JPM bear tenet that a primary-customer move to a **PCB-based backplane** could cut per-rack content (JPM, 2025-12-15) as well as NVDA's later Kyber orthogonal-backplane-PCB direction. Cross-reference: the same source's NVDA-side ramp/TCO/reliability numbers were filed to [NVDA.md](NVDA.md) as historical base-rate context. No PT/rating/estimate superseded. |
| 2026-07-24 | ⭐ ANET | **/run-inbox — 3 SemiAnalysis sources (1 dedup-with-net-new, 1 NEW-light, 1 BACKFILL). Additive: no PT, rating or estimate superseded (JPM OW $175 / MS OW $190 / TD Cowen Buy / SIG Positive $160 / Citi Buy $176 all retained).** **DEDUP WITH NET-NEW — SemiAnalysis "Meta's Infrastructure Team Needs A Culture Reset" (07-21):** a 07-21 row was already on the page (added on the 07-21 pass, under the `Meta_-_semianalysis.html` slug) but it stopped at the DSF *description* — the two bespoke SKUs (7700R4C + 7720R4) sole-sourced from Arista with Meta as effectively the single anchor buyer, "likely sub-scale on cost structure," on Broadcom Jericho3-AI + Ramon3 — and at "Meta quickly pivoted away." **What was missing, and is what actually matters, is the NSF half:** NSF boxes run shallow-buffer **Tomahawk 5** and are sold by "**virtually every major switch vendor such as Arista, as well as white-box vendors like Celestica, meaning better cost optimization and a diversity of solutions**"; SemiAnalysis models **DSF ~11% more expensive than NSF** and notes DSF "forces Meta to rely only on a single vendor, whereas NSF brings vendor diversity"; **Prometheus and Hyperion now deploy a vast majority of NSF instances**; DSF survives only where **MTIA** is used (NIC integrated on-chip) while GB300 runs Nvidia NICs → NSF, so both co-exist. Also net-new: Meta ran DSF under its own **FBOSS** NOS and "could have arguably gone for Arista **EOS**" but chose FBOSS for integration flexibility — i.e. the software/ASP premium at this account was never fully ANET's. **The framing was written deliberately, not smoothed:** this is **not a lost customer** (ANET sells the NSF boxes) — it is the **loss of a sole-source, high-content, deep-buffer position with a single anchor buyer in favour of a commoditised shallow-buffer box that whiteboxes also sell**, i.e. a **mix/ASP and competitive-intensity read, not a volume-loss read**. Framed on the page as (i) the fixed cost of the vertical-integration advantage the bull case rests on (~0.3-0.5% of revenue), and (ii) the reason Baltra and the AVGO ~2029/30 AI-infrastructure ramp are multi-year non-switchable commitments whose design cost scales faster than program count. **(2) SemiAnalysis / STEEL "Is SMIC N+3's Metal Pitch Smaller than Intel 18A's?" Critically, SemiAnalysis attributes the gap to **node access rather than design** — "Apple and Qualcomm build on N4 and N3P, which are denser and sit on a better voltage-frequency curve, giving them a larger transistor budget and more performance per watt" — which makes the silicon moat explicitly contingent on continued leading-edge TSMC allocation, tying it to the FY27 ~10%-including-Apple wafer-price read and the advanced-node-supply risk already on the page. Sept-Q iPhone revenue **~8% above Street**, December **-5% below**.<br>**The two items MS calls "critical to a re-rating," both risks:** (1) **June-Q Services +13.3% y/y vs consensus 14.6% (-130bps)** on weak App Store — **developer revenue +3.2% y/y, ~460bps of deceleration from March**; Sept Q **+12.7% vs Street 13.2%**, with **July MTD App Store developer revenue at just +1.0% y/y (Sensor Tower)**, partly offset by ~1pt from Apple Music price rises. (2) **Sept-Q gross margin: MSe 46.8% vs Street 47.4%**, guide expected at **46.5-47.5% = technically a GM guide-down**, on component-cost inflation, less low-cost component inventory and limited price-hike protection — implying **Products GM -70bps q/q against a historical +130bps**. MS believes both are already in line with buy-side expectations, hence "neutral setup" rather than "risk." **Mac is supply-capped** on **advanced-node SoC availability** (Mac mini, Mac Studio, MacBook Neo) → June/Sept shipment forecasts cut **11%/8%**, though **MacBook Neo output is ramping toward 8-10M units in 2H26 vs an original 6-8M plan**; iPad prices +$100-150. Net Sept-Q EPS still **5% above Street**, June Q possibly **$1.90-1.95** on Apple's usual upside surprise; "constructive over a 12-month horizon… **but don't expect much, if any, follow-through post-earnings**" after outperforming the market by 7pts over four weeks.<br>**@markgurman (07-22/24) — net-new, and the important part is the cost/allocation gate:** an **end-to-end Mac overhaul** (M6 MacBook Pro + new iMacs this year; redesigned MacBook Pros with **touch and OLED**; new MacBook Air and MacBook Neo; OLED iMac and OLED MacBook Air later; a MacBook Neo on **A19 Pro with more memory**) — but **"M5 Pro + M6 Mac mini and M5 Max + M5 Ultra Mac Studio… configurations and release timing are fairly tied to the memory shortage. Current models don't ship for three months."** This adds **DRAM/NAND cost and allocation as a second supply gate alongside advanced-node SoCs**, which the page previously only carried as an SoC constraint — cross-read [[MU]] / [[SAMSUNG]] / [[SKHYNIX]]. Gurman is also bullish on the foldable after using the Z Fold 8 ("**it's going to be a home run**, even at over $2000"), which cuts against MS's slower-foldable-ramp Sept-Q build assumption — a two-sided read left unresolved per wiki convention.<br>**UBS · David Vogt First Reads (07-23/24):** **T-Mobile upgrade rate 2.6%, +10bps y/y**; Verizon showing **higher retention and lower subsidies**. Mixed carrier channel into the print (AT&T was -10bps y/y on 07-22), with the **lower Verizon promotional subsidy** the more relevant variable for iPhone sell-through elasticity given the assumed $200 price increase.<br>**Edits:** 3 new Intra-quarter Full-log rows (07-23 MS preview, 07-23/24 Gurman, 07-24 UBS). |
